=== 1648311b-4c7a-4d07-9fb6-5fc4a06432a4 [assistant] ts= ===
Acknowledged — workspace is now `none` (no folder open). I made no edits after the first response and have nothing in-flight to compact.  ## On the actual question  There's no Cursor setting for "auto-compact at N tokens mid-task." The reasons:  1. **Cursor does compaction agent-side.** The `/compact` slash command (and the auto-compaction that fires when the window fills) runs inside your conversation; the assistant has no preference to tune. 2. **The trigger isn't a token slider — it's the model window.** Compaction kicks in when the conversation is *about* to overflow the context window Cursor negotiated for the current model (e.g., Sonnet 4.5 ≈ 200K, but the negotiated limit is lower). A "150K" preference would just delay — not eliminate — the same compaction event. 3. **There's no user-facing setting in Cursor Desktop for this.** Settings.json has `cursor.composer.*` keys, but I've never seen one exposed for compaction threshold. Worth confirming in Cursor → Settings → search "compact" / "context" (Workspace Trust and Privacy section is where any future toggle would appear).  ## Practical habits instead of a setting  Given context-the-heavy-tasks nature of this workspace (firm

Three honest pictures:  ### Option A — Scalar per-axis Kalman (1 state per axis) ``` state per axis:    b ∈ ℝ           (just the bias) prediction:        b̂_{k+1} = b̂_k measurement:       z_k = x_meas − b̂_k     (innovation) update:            b̂_{k+1} = b̂_k + K·(z_k − b̂_k) ``` This is **exactly** what your existing v3 OF-bias estimator does (`b ← b + K·(z − b)`). === 18c1dfa0-7c30-4e7e-8ebe-5e66b5ac1037 [assistant] ts= ===
Strong set of questions, and the EKF-augmented-MRAC one is genuinely interesting for your thesis — it's not just an implementation detail, it's a research direction. Let me take each in turn, then close with a clean recommendation.  ## 1. EKF-augmented MRAC and covariance-informed adaptation  This is a real and active research area, and your intuition is exactly right. The short version:  **What "covariance-informed adaptation" actually means**  Every EKF maintains a covariance matrix `P` that quantifies "how uncertain is the filter about each state right now?" The diagonal of `P` is the variance per state; the off-diagonals are the cross-correlations between states. When the EKF gets a new measurement, it computes the **innovation** `ν = z − H·x̂` (what the sensor actually said, minus what the filter predicted it would say) and the **innovation covariance** `S = H·P·Hᵀ + R`. The ratio `νᵀ·S⁻¹·ν` is the **Normalized Innovation Squared (NIS)** — a chi-squared-distributed test statistic.  The key fact: **NIS tells you whether the model and the world agree.** If NIS is close to its expected value (≈ sensor count), the model is right. If NIS is much larger, something is unmodeled — a d

Excellent questions — these are the kind that move a project from "it works" to "I understand why it works." Let me answer each carefully because they're real engineering points, not stylistic ones.  ## 1. "Adaption should be guided by how much we trust the measurements, and we don't adapt to noise or incorrect bias — and this trust measure is enhanced with more sources."  This is the right mental model. Two concrete formulations, in order of difficulty:  **The principled formulation (what you'd want to cite in a thesis):**  The adaptive gain is bounded by the **posterior covariance** of the EKF state. Concretely, at every tick:  ``` Γ_eff(k) = Γ_max · diag(P_attitude(k)) / (diag(P_attitude(k)) + ε) ```  The interpretation: when the EKF is confident (small P diagonal), Γ_eff approaches Γ_max (full adaptation). When the EKF is uncertain (large P diagonal — measurements disagree with the model), Γ_eff approaches 0 (no adaptation, because we don't trust the measurement that would drive it).  The "more sources = more trust" point you made is exactly the case: each new measurement source (OF, GPS, baro, vision) reduces the corresponding `P` diagonal through the Kalman update step, which

Let me write the smallest reasonable version:  - **Predict**: `x_pred = F*x + B*u` where `F ≈ I + dt*` (constant-velocity), `B*u = [a_body - b_a, 0, -g]` (9-vector) - **P_pred = F*P*F^T + Q` - **Update**: `K = P*H^T*(H*P*H^T + R)^-1` (scalar R so 2×2 inversion) - **x = x + K*y`, `P = (I-K*H)*P` - **Body-frame accel as measurement** maps to v_body (since in static hover, gravity-removed accel ≈ 0 means v_body should be 0; residual = -lin_acc_xy; this drives v_body toward zero AND learns b_a via the bias-as-augmented-state trick). ## Telemetry / Comms Seam Report  GS protocol version: **14** (`GS_PROTO_VERSION` in `global_declare.h:48`)  ---  ### 1. Telemetry Frame Types  All frames share the wire format `[0xAA][0xBB][type][LEN_hi][LEN_lo][MAX_NUM_BASIS][payload][CRC8]`, where CRC8 is XOR over every byte from index 2 onward. Frame C (0x06) uses CRC16-CCITT instead.  #### Frame 0x01 — Frame A (100 Hz, alternates with Frame B) **Conditions:** `motor_test_active==0 && mrac_flags.id_frame_on==0 && mrac_flags.of_frame_on==0 && (frame_counter % 5 != 0)`  **Wire size:** 6 header + 41 payload + 1 CRC = **48 bytes**  | Offset | Name | Type | Units | Notes | |--------|------|------|-------|-------| | 0 | `mrac.pitch.e` | f32 | rad/s | Tracking error e = x - xm | | 4 | `mrac.pitch.u_ad` | f32 | Nm/N | Adaptive MRAC effort | | 8 | `mrac.roll.e` | f32 | rad/s | | | 12 | `mrac.roll.u_ad` | f32 | Nm/N | | | 16 | `mrac.yaw.e` | f32 | rad/s | | | 20 | `mrac.yaw.u_ad` | f32 | Nm/N | | | 24 | `mrac.z.e` | f32 | rad/s | | | 28 | `mrac.z.u_ad` | f32 | Nm/N | | | 32 | `status.arm` | u8 | — | 0=Disarmed, 1=Armed | | 33 | `status.flymode` | u8 | — | 0=DangerousStop, 1=SDK | | 34 | `status.sbus_lost` | u8 | — | 1=RC link dead | | 35

Strong set of questions, and the EKF-augmented-MRAC one is genuinely interesting for your thesis — it's not just an implementation detail, it's a research direction. Let me take each in turn, then close with a clean recommendation.  ## 1. EKF-augmented MRAC and covariance-informed adaptation  This is a real and active research area, and your intuition is exactly right. The short version:  **What "covariance-informed adaptation" actually means**  Every EKF maintains a covariance matrix `P` that quantifies "how uncertain is the filter about each state right now?" The diagonal of `P` is the variance per state; the off-diagonals are the cross-correlations between states. When the EKF gets a new measurement, it computes the **innovation** `ν = z − H·x̂` (what the sensor actually said, minus what the filter predicted it would say) and the **innovation covariance** `S = H·P·Hᵀ + R`. The ratio `νᵀ·S⁻¹·ν` is the **Normalized Innovation Squared (NIS)** — a chi-squared-distributed test statistic.  The key fact: **NIS tells you whether the model and the world agree.** If NIS is close to its expected value (≈ sensor count), the model is right. If NIS is much larger, something is unmodeled — a d

Excellent questions — these are the kind that move a project from "it works" to "I understand why it works." Let me answer each carefully because they're real engineering points, not stylistic ones.  ## 1. "Adaption should be guided by how much we trust the measurements, and we don't adapt to noise or incorrect bias — and this trust measure is enhanced with more sources."  This is the right mental model. Two concrete formulations, in order of difficulty:  **The principled formulation (what you'd want to cite in a thesis):**  The adaptive gain is bounded by the **posterior covariance** of the EKF state. Concretely, at every tick:  ``` Γ_eff(k) = Γ_max · diag(P_attitude(k)) / (diag(P_attitude(k)) + ε) ```  The interpretation: when the EKF is confident (small P diagonal), Γ_eff approaches Γ_max (full adaptation). When the EKF is uncertain (large P diagonal — measurements disagree with the model), Γ_eff approaches 0 (no adaptation, because we don't trust the measurement that would drive it).  The "more sources = more trust" point you made is exactly the case: each new measurement source (OF, GPS, baro, vision) reduces the corresponding `P` diagonal through the Kalman update step, which

## Root Cause Analysis: Velocity PID is PURE Integral (Ki only)  **The evidence:**  From `pid.c:24-25`, the velocity loop (`locxsPID`/`locysPID`) gains are: ```c // locxs  Kp=3.0  Ki=0  Kd=6.0 // locys  Kp=3.0  Ki=0  Kd=6.0 ``` Ki=0. Kd=6.0. Kp=3.0 but since the loop is `error × Kp` where `error = Des - FB`, and **Des=0** in OF hold, the P term produces zero output when the drone is stationary (error=0). The entire velocity correction is from the D term on velocity changes.  ---  ### Why this causes oscillation:  **The loop is a pure D controller with zero setpoint.** It has no damping from proportional action and no integral to eliminate steady-state error. Every time the OF sensor produces a new velocity measurement, it computes:  ``` U = Kd × (velocity - 0) = 6.0 × velocity ```  This is an undamped, high-gain D controller. The instant the drone starts moving in any direction, the D term spikes and creates a large lean command. The lean accelerates the drone in the *same* direction it was already moving (because the attitude loop has its own dynamics), which then causes the velocity to increase further, which triggers another spike. **No restoring force exists between velocity me

Here's what the PCB pinout diagram (`NOTE/pcb.txt`) tells us:  **Occupied headers (motor side):** - TIM3 CH1-CH4 → PA6, PA7, PB0, PB1 (4 motor outputs) - TIM4 CH1-CH2 → PB6, PB7 (servo outputs)  **Free headers (opposite side):** - TIM2 CH1 → PA5 - TIM2 CH2 → PB3 - TIM2 CH3 → PB10 - TIM2 CH4 → PB11 - (Plus GND and 5V pins)  These are **PWM-capable pins** — they're set up as alternate-function timer outputs. The TIM2 channels are already initialized in PWM mode, which means they output a PWM signal, not a static GPIO input. The `PWM_TIM2_Init()` function configures all TIM2 channels as PWM outputs, so they can't be used as simple digital inputs for your IR sensor OUT without disabling/repurposing the timer.  **My recommendation stands: use `PC0` or `PC1` on the J1 header.**  But if you want to use one of the free PWM headers (TIM2 side), you have two options:  1. **Reuse PA5** (TIM2 CH1): The `PWM_TIM2_Init()` function initializes it, but the TIM_OCInitStructure for CH1 has `TIM_OutputState = TIM_OutputState_Enable` — meaning it's actively driving PWM. Here's the complete analysis:  **The free 4-channel header (TIM2 side)** maps to: **PA5, PB3, PB10, PB11**  The good news: `PWM_TIM2_Init()` is **dead code — never called**. This is almost certainly a hardware/wiring issue. The firmware init is identical for all 4 pins — if one works, the code is fine. Common causes:  1. **PCB trace routing** — not all J1 pins PC2–PC5 may be routed on your specific FC board. Some pins may share a trace or be connected to something else entirely. Check with a multimeter continuity test: probe each PC pin at the MCU leg, then at the J1 header pad.  2. **Solder bridge / dry joint** — PC2 might have a good joint while PC3–PC5 are cold joints or bridged to adjacent pins.  3. **Sensor wiring** — are all 4 sensors wired identically (OUT → PC pin, GND → common GND, VCC → 5V)? A loose or swapped wire on 3 of them would explain it.  4. **Sensor supply sag** — 4 IR sensors drawing ~40–80 mA total may be drooping the 5V rail if the FC's 5V supply is weak, especially under load. === b1360896-428d-4abc-ac0a-ad2d4504a7fd [assistant] ts= ===
You're right. The filter doesn't fix the underlying problem — it just makes the **stuck** readings more reliable. If a sensor is genuinely being driven wrong by another sensor on the bus, you'll get a steady wrong answer instead of a glitchy wrong answer.  **Independent RPM** requires the sensors to NOT share an electrical fight. Three firmware-side options that actually achieve that, ranked by effort:  ### Option 1 — One-by-one enable (recommended, ~20 lines) Most IR reflective modules have an **EN** pin. If yours do, drive them from PC8/PC9/PC10/PC11 (or any spare GPIOs) and only power one sensor at a time:  ``` turn on sensor 1, read, turn off turn on sensor 2, read, turn off turn on sensor 3, read, turn off turn on sensor 4, read, turn off ```  Round-robin at 1 kHz → each sensor sampled at 250 Hz. Way faster than your propeller (max ~10,000 RPM = 167 Hz per blade pass, but you'll only care about slow RPM changes anyway).  ### Option 2 — Software time-multiplexing without EN pin If there's no EN pin, but the OUT is open-collector, you can use **4 GPIOs as the VCC supply** instead of tying VCC to battery: - Configure 4 spare pins as open-drain outputs - Drive them HIGH one at a t

PA4 is your only ADC pin (channel 4, battery sense). **15 channels free.** TCRT5000 OUT analog output (the exposed pin on the breakout, usually A0) can be wired to PA5, PA6, PA7, PB0 — four free ADC channels, **one sensor each, no electrical sharing.**  ### The plan  1. **Wire change (you):** TCRT5000 modules have **OUT (digital)** and **A0 (analog)** pins. Cut/disconnect the OUT trace or just leave it floating (you won't read it anymore). Wire each sensor's **A0** to a separate FC ADC pin. 2. **Firmware change (me):** Add PA5, PA6, PA7, PB0 as ADC channels, sample each at 1 kHz, threshold ~half-scale to give you the same 0/1 RPM-pulse signal you're getting now — but **independently**. 3. **Backward compat:** Existing `IRSensorMotor_IsDetected()` calls keep working — I'll rewire them internally to read the ADC thresholded. No caller change.  ### Honest trade-off  ADC at 480-cycle sample time per channel × 4 channels = ~92 µs to read all 4. That's ~10.8 kHz aggregate, ~2.7 kHz per sensor. **More than enough** for any RPM (even 60,000 RPM = 1 kHz blade pass). Good catch — that size mismatch is real. UART pads on FCs are usually **2.54 mm pitch through-holes** (or SMT test pads), and **DuPont jumper clips don't fit**. Here are 3 ways around it, cheapest first.  ---  ## Option A — Buy the right-size female-to-female jumpers (~$2, no soldering)  Search AliExpress: **"2.54mm female to female dupont jumper wire"**. These have 2.54 mm female sockets that **clip straight onto 2.54 mm male header pins**.  If your UART pads are **male header pins sticking up** (very common on FCs), these clip on directly. The "TX/RX labels" on a custom FC often mean **2.54 mm male pins soldered into the board** — that's exactly what these jumpers fit.  👉 **Try this first.** Get a 40-wire ribbon of female-to-female jumpers, cut/break off 4 wires. Each sensor's OUT wire clips onto one UART pin. Total cost ~$2.  ---  ## Option B — Buy a "pin header breakout board" (~$2, no soldering)  Search AliExpress: **"2.54mm pitch screw terminal block breakout"** or **"4-pin 2.54mm screw terminal to Dupont"**. === b1360896-428d-4abc-ac0a-ad2d4504a7fd [assistant] ts= ===
**Build succeeded — 0 errors, 59 warnings, code size 71960 bytes. You're good to flash.** 🎉  ---  ## Warning analysis (in order of importance)  ### 🟢 Harmless — don't touch (54 of 59)  **1. The `mov r0, r0` warnings** — `global_declare.h:286` and `:306` (in every file) - **What it is**: a `__asm volatile("mov r0, r0\n")` no-op stub used to defeat Cortex-M4 optimization (it's a "compiler barrier"). - **Why ARMCC warns**: it wants you to declare R0 as a variable to silence the warning. But this is a standard idiom — the warning is spurious. - **Risk**: zero. The pattern is intentional and correct. - **Fix (optional)**: change to `"mov r0, r0\t\n"` (with tab) — ARMCC's `__asm` parser sometimes misinterprets the bare version. Or wrap it in a macro that ARMCC doesn't parse. **I would not bother** — leave it.  **2. The `#1-D: last line of file ends without a newline`** — `rpm.h:87` (in rpm.c, BSP.c, send_data.c, stm32f4xx_it.c) - **What it is**: missing trailing newline. - **Risk**: zero. Just pedantic. - **Fix (optional)**: open `rpm.h`, hit End-of-line at line 87, press Enter once. 30 seconds.  ### 🟡 Worth fixing (3 warnings)  **3. `sysid.c(163): pointless comparison of unsigned intege

Here's the report.  ---  # 🎯 Dashboard Dead — Diagnosis Report  You were right that wiring isn't the issue. **The link is alive but the dashboard is starving.** Here's why.  ## The 30-second answer  Your firmware v13 now emits **Frame A + Frame C back-to-back in the same UART4 buffer**. The bridge's `_rx_loop` is **stateful** — it walks `[0xAA][0xBB][type][LEN_hi][LEN_lo][MAX_NUM_BASIS]` *one byte at a time* with no skip-on-mismatch. When Frame A's tail CRC8 byte happens to equal `0xAA`, the bridge thinks a new sync started **inside Frame A's payload** and **desyncs**. From that point on, every frame is mis-aligned and the dashboard reads zeros.  **Estimated drop rate with v13 layout: ~30–60 % of Frame A frames lost**, depending on which float bit pattern lands on `0xAA`. Frame C (50 B + 16-bit payload containing 0xAA) makes it worse. Net result: `Link: -- (ID idle)` because the ID frame is too rare to clear the 1-second freshness check.  ---  ## The evidence trail  | Where | What I saw | Implication | |---|---|---| | `TASK/send_data.c:336-337,565-641` | Firmware writes `Buf

=== ee3ca6b7-f85d-4ab9-9741-9ef46cb22b1e [assistant] ts= ===
Interesting — Python's `(168e6 * 60) & 0xFFFFFFFF` doesn't wrap because Python ints are unbounded; that line of output shows the post-wrap value. The auditor's claim "BUGGY 60U * 168e6 = 1,490,065,408" matches what ARMCC produces (the C `60U * (uint32_t)168000000` wraps in C because both operands are u32, but the value `1680000000` happens to exceed `2^31` and wraps to `1490065408` mod 2^32; Python's `& 0xFFFFFFFF` emulates this wrap correctly here).  Both fixes check out: - **FIXED RPM_MIN_PERIOD_CYCLES = 504000** (was 74503 under the wrap). - **FIXED RPM @ glitch boundary = 20000** and **FIXED RPM @ 6000 RPM = 6000** — the auditor's two sanity cases.  Now verify C89-locals-at-top is still respected in the modified ISR (no new locals introduced, all 4 still at top):

Three honest pictures:  ### Option A — Scalar per-axis Kalman (1 state per axis) ``` state per axis:    b ∈ ℝ           (just the bias) prediction:        b̂_{k+1} = b̂_k measurement:       z_k = x_meas − b̂_k     (innovation) update:            b̂_{k+1} = b̂_k + K·(z_k − b̂_k) ``` This is **exactly** what your existing v3 OF-bias estimator does (`b ← b + K·(z − b)`). It's an LPF with a tunable time constant — `K` is small (≈1e-4 in your code) so it tracks slowly. **Cost: ~3 multiplies and 3 adds per axis per tick.** What it can do: track a slow bias drift during a long hover. === ff9e71e8-87b2-4d3b-af99-9135c400afcc [assistant] ts= ===
Strong set of questions, and the EKF-augmented-MRAC one is genuinely interesting for your thesis — it's not just an implementation detail, it's a research direction. Let me take each in turn, then close with a clean recommendation.  ## 1. EKF-augmented MRAC and covariance-informed adaptation  This is a real and active research area, and your intuition is exactly right. The short version:  **What "covariance-informed adaptation" actually means**  Every EKF maintains a covariance matrix `P` that quantifies "how uncertain is the filter about each state right now?" The diagonal of `P` is the variance per state; the off-diagonals are the cross-correlations between states. When the EKF gets a new measurement, it computes the **innovation** `ν = z − H·x̂` (what the sensor actually said, minus what the filter predicted it would say) and the **innovation covariance** `S = H·P·Hᵀ + R`. The ratio `νᵀ·S⁻¹·ν` is the **Normalized Innovation Squared (NIS)** — a chi-squared-distributed test statistic.  The key fact: **NIS tells you whether the model and the world agree.** If NIS is close to its expected value (≈ sensor count), the model is right. If NIS is much larger, something is unmodeled — a d

=== ff9e71e8-87b2-4d3b-af99-9135c400afcc [assistant] ts= ===
Excellent questions — these are the kind that move a project from "it works" to "I understand why it works." Let me answer each carefully because they're real engineering points, not stylistic ones.  ## 1. "Adaption should be guided by how much we trust the measurements, and we don't adapt to noise or incorrect bias — and this trust measure is enhanced with more sources."  This is the right mental model. Two concrete formulations, in order of difficulty:  **The principled formulation (what you'd want to cite in a thesis):**  The adaptive gain is bounded by the **posterior covariance** of the EKF state. Concretely, at every tick:  ``` Γ_eff(k) = Γ_max · diag(P_attitude(k)) / (diag(P_attitude(k)) + ε) ```  The interpretation: when the EKF is confident (small P diagonal), Γ_eff approaches Γ_max (full adaptation). When the EKF is uncertain (large P diagonal — measurements disagree with the model), Γ_eff approaches 0 (no adaptation, because we don't trust the measurement that would drive it).  The "more sources = more trust" point you made is exactly the case: each new measurement source (OF, GPS, baro, vision) reduces the corresponding `P` diagonal through the Kalman update step, which

=== ff9e71e8-87b2-4d3b-af99-9135c400afcc [assistant] ts= ===
Types are correct. Both object files (`StabilizerTask.o` and `send_data.o`) compiled clean. The linker failing to resolve a symbol that exists in a compiled file is almost always a **stale object file** — the build system reused an old `.o` that was compiled before the definitions existed, or the `.o` wasn't regenerated after a rebuild.  The fix: **Clean + Rebuild** in uVision: 1. **Project → Clean Target** (deletes all `.o` and `.axf` files) 2. **Project → Rebuild All Target Files**  This forces every `.c` to recompile from scratch, including `StabilizerTask.c` which will emit the `s_cal_hot`/`s_cal_trim` symbols into `StabilizerTask.o` fresh.  If after a clean rebuild you still get the 2 errors, something deeper is wrong — but 99% of the time this is a stale-object issue.  ---  ### 2. GUI test harness + clone vs. build  Here is a direct answer to your question:  **Option A: Clone + customize (recommended)** **Do this.** The reason is straightforward: a flight-controller GUI has no novel UI challenges.
